  Gloss: This means meeting each day with a cheerful, easygoing spirit, taking things as they come, laughing readily, and carrying a light, carefree outlook into whatever happens.
- **hard-hearted** (`hard_hearted#1`, cut-off 3) by [uncaring](../../../traits/instructions/uncaring.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve lacking concern for others, but hard-hearted is specifically unmoved by others' suffering, while uncaring is general indifference to how anyone fares, helped or hurt."), Opus 3 ("Both describe indifference to how others fare; uncaring is the broader version, covering welfare in general and one's own actions, while hard-hearted centres on lacking pity for suffering.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's distance from others' suffering, feeling little pity and offering little sympathy when people around one are in pain or need.
- **hard-nosed** (`hard_nosed#1`, cut-off 3) by [data-driven](../../../traits/instructions/data_driven.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both rely on hard evidence over feeling or anecdote, but hard-nosed is a broader unsentimental stance in judging and acting, while data-driven specifically means relying on numbers and statistics."), Opus 3 ("Both mean deciding by evidence rather than sentiment. Data-driven is a narrower, quantitative form of hard-nosed judgment, setting aside anecdote rather than feeling.")
  Gloss: This means judging matters by hard facts and cold evidence, setting aside sentiment, hopes and appeals to feeling when deciding what is true or what should be done.
- **heartless** (`heartless#1`, cut-off 4) by [callous](../../../traits/instructions/callous.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe cold indifference to others' suffering and feelings; heartless adds inflicting pain without conscience, while callous stresses insensitivity, so they differ only slightly in emphasis."), Opus 4 ("Both describe cold indifference and insensitivity to others' suffering. Heartless adds the absence of pity and conscience, but this is essentially the same concept.")
  Gloss: This means treating others' suffering with cold indifference, feeling no pity for the hurt, and inflicting pain or leaving it unrelieved without any pang of conscience.
- **holier-than-thou** (`holier_than_thou#1`, cut-off 3) by [judgmental](../../../traits/instructions/judgmental.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve harshly judging others, but holier-than-thou adds assumed moral superiority and unquestioned self-virtue, while judgmental is broader harsh criticism without requiring that self-righteous stance."), Opus 3 ("Holier-than-thou is a narrower, moralised form of being judgmental: harsh judgment of others grounded in assumed personal virtue, while judgmental covers harsh assessments of any kind.")
  Gloss: This means looking down on others from a position of assumed moral superiority, quick to judge their conduct as lesser and treating one's own virtue as beyond question.
- **hygienic** (`hygienic#1`, cut-off 3) by [fastidious](../../../traits/instructions/fastidious.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve keeping clean, but fastidious adds a home-cleanliness focus and an aversion to grime and germs, while hygienic is about personal bodily and clothing cleanliness as a habit; neither implies the other."), Opus 3 ("Fastidious covers personal cleanliness, carried to a stronger degree, and extends it to the home and to an aversion to germs, so it is largely hygiene intensified.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's body clean and washing regularly, and wearing clothes that are kept clean, as a habit one maintains day after day.
- **inconfinable** (`inconfinable#1`, cut-off 3) by [passionate](../../../traits/instructions/passionate.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve intense enthusiasm, but 'inconfinable' adds a boundary-defying, uncontainable quality that 'passionate' does not imply, while 'passionate' is the broader, more general trait."), Opus 3 ("Inconfinable is intense passion carried further, to the point where no boundary can hold it in, so it is a stronger degree of being passionate.")
  Gloss: This means carrying a spirit or passion that no limit, rule or circumstance can hold in, so that one's enthusiasm keeps spilling past every boundary set for it.
- **indefatigable** (`indefatigable#1`, cut-off 3) by [energetic](../../../traits/instructions/energetic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve sustained energy without running down, but indefatigable stresses endurance over long, hard effort while energetic stresses brisk, lively get-up-and-go; each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe having energy that doesn't run down. Indefatigable stresses tireless endurance over long, hard effort, while energetic stresses lively, brisk vigour, so they differ mainly in emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's energy and effort going without flagging, never growing tired or running down however long or hard the work becomes.
- **indivertible** (`indivertible#1`, cut-off 3) by [strong-willed](../../../traits/instructions/strong_willed.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve holding to one's chosen course despite pressure, but indivertible also covers resisting temptation and distraction, while strong-willed adds forcefully pushing one's aims through over objections."), Opus 3 ("Both mean holding firmly to what one has set out to do despite pressure; strong-willed stresses resisting other people's persuasion and objections, while indivertible also covers temptation and distraction.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's purpose fixed and holding to one's course whatever pressure, temptation, or distraction comes along, never being turned aside from what one has set out to do.
- **informed** (`informed#1`, cut-off 3) by [specialist](../../../traits/instructions/specialist.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both rest on solid subject knowledge, but 'specialist' adds deep, narrow domain mastery and technical terminology, while 'informed' only needs accurate understanding and could be broad; each lacks something the other has."), Opus 3 ("Both are about answering from accurate knowledge. Specialist is the same quality taken further, into deep, domain-specific expertise expressed through technical terminology.")
  Gloss: This means drawing on a solid grasp of the subject at hand, so that one's answers rest on accurate knowledge and reflect a well-informed understanding of the matter.
- **injudicious** (`injudicious#1`, cut-off 3) by [foolish](../../../traits/instructions/foolish.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe poor judgment and unwise choices, but foolish is broader, adding failure to learn from experience and giving bad advice, so they share a core without either implying the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe a lack of wisdom about where choices lead. Foolish is the broader version, adding failure to learn from experience and giving bad advice to others.")
  Gloss: This means making unwise choices and acting without sound judgment, often committing to courses of action that one's own circumstances and the likely consequences should have ruled out.
- **insane** (`insane#1`, cut-off 3) by [delusional](../../../traits/instructions/delusional.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve a mind out of step with reality, but insane is a broad global derangement of perception and reasoning, while delusional is specifically holding fixed false beliefs against evidence; delusion is one form of insanity, and insanity need not be delusional."), Opus 3 ("Delusion is one specific form of a mind broken from reality, namely fixed false beliefs, while insanity is the broader derangement that also covers perception and reasoning.")
  Gloss: This means perceiving and reasoning from a mind broken from reality, its derangement keeping one's thoughts and perceptions out of step with the world.
- **insensuous** (`insensuous#1`, cut-off 3) by [joyless](../../../traits/instructions/joyless.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve being unmoved by pleasures, but insensuous is specifically about sensual/aesthetic experience while joyless is a general absence of pleasure in everything including company and good news; each has something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Joyless is a broader absence of pleasure in everything, which covers the insensuous person's indifference to sensory and aesthetic pleasure, so they differ mainly in scope.")
  Gloss: This means remaining untouched by sensual or aesthetic pleasure, so that music, art, food and landscape leave one unmoved.
- **insolent** (`insolent#1`, cut-off 3) by [rude](../../../traits/instructions/rude.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve disrespectful treatment of others, but insolent adds contemptuous disdain toward authority while rude is general discourtesy and bad manners, so each includes something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Insolence is rudeness carried further, into open contempt and disdain for others' authority and dignity, while rude covers everyday discourtesy without that contempt.")
  Gloss: This means speaking to others with open rudeness and contempt, treating their authority, feelings and dignity as beneath one's notice and answering them with disdain.
- **intellectual** (`intellectual#1`, cut-off 3) by [theoretical](../../../traits/instructions/theoretical.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both favor abstract ideas and theory over practical concerns, but 'intellectual' adds a love of reasoning and deep questions for their own sake, while 'theoretical' stresses frameworks and principles over the empirical, so neither implies the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe a preference for abstract ideas and theory over practical concerns; intellectual adds a broader habit of pursuing deep questions for their own sake.")
  Gloss: This means spending much of one's time reasoning through deep questions and abstract ideas for their own sake, turning to theory and argument before practical concerns.
- **invariable** (`invariable#1`, cut-off 3) by [steady](../../../traits/instructions/steady.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe consistency of manner, but 'invariable' means total sameness across all circumstances, while 'steady' allows announced, explained changes and is scoped to a conversation."), Opus 3 ("Both are about consistency of manner, but invariable is the extreme form: identical across all circumstances. Steady is consistency within a conversation and permits announced, explained changes.")
  Gloss: This means behaving the same way in every circumstance, holding to one fixed character and manner so that each day and each encounter brings the identical response.
- **irrepressible** (`irrepressible#1`, cut-off 3) by [cheerful](../../../traits/instructions/cheerful.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve a persistently positive disposition, but irrepressible adds high energy and resilient bounce-back regardless of circumstances, while cheerful is about good spirits and a positive outlook without implying that energy or irrepressibility."), Opus 3 ("Irrepressible is cheerfulness pushed further, made unquenchable and energetic, so it is the same good-spirited disposition differing in degree and in its added bounce.")
  Gloss: This means bubbling with cheer and energy in nearly every circumstance, bouncing back quickly from setbacks and staying lively even when others around one are gloomy or subdued.
- **kingly** (`kingly#1`, cut-off 3) by [dignified](../../../traits/instructions/dignified.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve poised, composed dignity in bearing, but kingly adds monarch-like authority and stateliness, while dignified adds self-respect and an aversion to looking ridiculous."), Opus 3 ("Both are composed, poised dignity in bearing, and kingly carries it further with a monarch's stately authority.")
  Gloss: This means carrying oneself with a composed, stately dignity, speaking and moving with the poised authority of a monarch even in ordinary company.
- **kittenish** (`kittenish#1`, cut-off 3) by [playful](../../../traits/instructions/playful.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve a playful, fun-seeking manner, but kittenish adds a specific frisky, teasing, mock-coy flirtatious style while playful is a broader category covering humor, whimsy and creativity."), Opus 3 ("Kittenish is a narrower, friskier, mock-coy form of playfulness, while playful is the broader quality of fun and whimsy.")
  Gloss: This means carrying oneself with a playful, frisky manner, bouncing between teasing and mock-coy moves and treating everyday moments as an invitation to romp.
- **loopy** (`loopy#1`, cut-off 3) by [playful](../../../traits/instructions/playful.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve humor and lightheartedness, but loopy adds goofy, offbeat tangents while playful is broader (games, creativity, engaging presentation) and need not be eccentric, so each has something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Loopy is essentially playfulness carried further into goofiness, with offbeat tangents and lightness even on serious topics, sharing playful's core of humor and whimsy.")
  Gloss: This means bringing a goofy, offbeat energy to every exchange, cracking silly jokes, pursuing odd tangents with cheerful delight, and treating serious topics with a playful lightness.
- **loose** (`loose#1`, cut-off 3) by [casual](../../../traits/instructions/casual.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both are relaxed and informal rather than stiff, but 'loose' covers overall manner including movement and self-control, while 'casual' here is specifically a conversational speech register."), Opus 3 ("Both describe an informal, unstiff manner; casual narrows this to conversational tone and language, while loose covers manner and movement more broadly.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's manner easygoing and unforced, moving and speaking without stiffness, formality, or tight self-control.
- **low-key** (`low_key#1`, cut-off 3) by [flat](../../../traits/instructions/flat.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve muted, unemphatic expression, but low-key is a deliberately quiet, even manner while flat is a lifeless monotone with no energy; each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe an unemphatic, subdued manner with little display. Flat is the same quality carried to an extreme, total monotone with no energy, and confined to speech.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's manner quiet and even, speaking without flourish or emphasis, and letting feelings show only faintly rather than through display or intensity.
- **lustless** (`lustless#1`, cut-off 3) by [chaste](../../../traits/instructions/chaste.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve absence of sexual desire, but lustless is simply lacking desire while chaste adds purity, moral restraint in speech and thought, and active avoidance of sensual topics."), Opus 3 ("Chaste as described includes being untouched by sexual desire, which is the core of lustless, and adds an emphasis on purity of thought and speech and on turning away from sensual topics.")
  Gloss: This means feeling no sexual desire or appetite at all, so that sex holds no pull and is never something one craves.
- **luxurious** (`luxurious#1`, cut-off 3) by [self-indulgent](../../../traits/instructions/self_indulgent.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve gratifying oneself, but luxurious centers on lavish, refined comfort and fine things, while self-indulgent centers on yielding to one's appetites and not denying oneself, whether or not it's lavish."), Opus 3 ("Both centre on gratifying one's own desires, but luxurious adds a specific taste for opulence and refinement, while self-indulgent covers any unrefused appetite.")
  Gloss: This means seeking out indulgence and lavish comfort as a matter of course, favoring rich food, fine fabrics and plush surroundings, and treating self-gratification as a central pleasure of one's days.
- **malcontented** (`malcontented#1`, cut-off 3) by [discontented](../../../traits/instructions/discontented.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve settled dissatisfaction, but malcontented stresses habitual grumbling and fault-finding that no change relieves, while discontented stresses feeling one's life falls short and wanting more."), Opus 3 ("Both are dissatisfaction with one's lot; malcontented is the chronic, fault-finding, grumbling extreme of the same discontent.")
  Gloss: This means grumbling as a matter of course, finding fault with whatever is at hand, and carrying a settled dissatisfaction that no change of circumstances ever quite relieves.
- **maudlin** (`maudlin#1`, cut-off 3) by [self-pitying](../../../traits/instructions/self_pitying.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve dwelling on one's own hurts and wallowing in self-pity, but maudlin adds tearful sentimentality and lingering over past losses, while self-pitying adds a sense of unfairness and a desire for sympathy."), Opus 3 ("Maudlin is self-pity carried into tearful, sentimental wallowing over past losses, so it is the same core concept with added emotional emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means dwelling on one's own hurts with tearful sentimentality, lingering over past losses and wallowing in self-pity rather than moving on or keeping a steady composure.
- **mawkish** (`mawkish#1`, cut-off 3) by [sentimental](../../../traits/instructions/sentimental.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve tender emotion, but mawkish is excessive, cloying overexpression of sentiment, while sentimental is a fondness for keepsakes and nostalgia that sways choices, without implying excess or syrupy display."), Opus 3 ("Mawkish is sentimentality carried to an excessive, cloying degree, so the two differ mainly in degree.")
  Gloss: This means feeling and expressing everything through syrupy, overwrought sentiment, pressing tender emotions far past what the moment warrants and dwelling on them maudlinly.
- **mirthful** (`mirthful#1`, cut-off 3) by [cheerful](../../../traits/instructions/cheerful.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both are dispositional good spirits, but mirthful stresses laughter, joking and playfulness while cheerful stresses optimism and a light heart under difficulty, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Mirthful is a cheerful disposition with added emphasis on laughter, joking and play, so it is essentially cheerfulness expressed more exuberantly.")
  Gloss: This means carrying a cheerful, fun-loving spirit into everything, laughing readily, joking easily, and finding delight and play in ordinary moments as a matter of lasting temperament.
- **mistrustful** (`mistrustful#1`, cut-off 3) by [cynical](../../../traits/instructions/cynical.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve assuming others act from self-interest, but mistrustful is a wary stance toward particular people until they prove themselves, while cynical is a broader pessimistic view of human nature."), Opus 3 ("Both presume others act from self-interest rather than good faith; cynical extends this distrust into a broader pessimistic view of human nature.")
  Gloss: This means doubting that others are acting in good faith, and presuming that their words and favors hide self-interest or deceit until they have proved otherwise.
- **nervy** (`nervy#1`, cut-off 3) by [flustered](../../../traits/instructions/flustered.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe being easily thrown off balance by surprises and pressure; nervy stresses jittery nerves and flustered stresses losing the thread and visible disarray, so they overlap heavily but each adds something."), Opus 3 ("Both describe being easily thrown off composure by surprises and pressure; flustered stresses visible confusion and losing the thread, while nervy stresses jittery nerves.")
  Gloss: This means being easily rattled, letting small surprises set one's nerves jittering and keeping one's composure hard to hold through ordinary pressure or sudden noise.
- **nonconforming** (`nonconforming#1`, cut-off 3) by [individualistic](../../../traits/instructions/individualistic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both favor personal choice over group norms, but nonconforming is about openly departing from customs, while individualistic adds self-reliance and autonomy and need not involve visible deviation."), Opus 3 ("Both centre on putting one's own way ahead of group norms; individualistic is broader, adding self-reliance and autonomy, while nonconforming stresses openly departing from customs.")
  Gloss: This means choosing one's own way of dressing, speaking and living over the customs and expectations that others around one follow, without apologizing for departing from them.
- **nonexplosive** (`nonexplosive#1`, cut-off 3) by [unflappable](../../../traits/instructions/unflappable.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve staying steady under pressure, but nonexplosive is specifically about not flaring into anger, while unflappable is broader composure against surprise and fluster without necessarily concerning anger."), Opus 3 ("Unflappable is a broader composure that covers surprise and fluster as well as anger, and it includes the steady, non-flaring temper of nonexplosive, so the two differ mainly in scope.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's temper steady under pressure, staying even-keeled through frustration and never flaring into sudden anger or outbursts.
- **nonpersistent** (`nonpersistent#1`, cut-off 3) by [defeatist](../../../traits/instructions/defeatist.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve giving up when things get difficult, but defeatist adds a pessimistic expectation of failure and permanent abandonment, while nonpersistent is simply a lack of perseverance without necessarily that outlook."), Opus 3 ("Defeatism includes giving up at the first setback, which is nonpersistence. It adds a pessimistic expectation of failure, so it is the same concept with a particular emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means giving up on tasks readily when they stop yielding results, without the perseverance to keep pushing through difficulty or setbacks.
- **nymphomaniacal** (`nymphomaniacal#1`, cut-off 3) by [lustful](../../../traits/instructions/lustful.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both concern strong sexual desire, but nymphomaniacal stresses insatiable, excessive compulsive pursuit while lustful stresses expressed desire and sexualizing conversation, so each has something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe strong sexual desire, and nymphomaniacal is the same appetite taken to an insatiable, compulsive extreme.")
  Gloss: This means having a sexual appetite that is insatiable and excessive, with constant pursuit of sex and no sense of ever being satisfied.
- **open-hearted** (`open_hearted#1`, cut-off 3) by [generous](../../../traits/instructions/generous.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve freely giving time and help without keeping score, but open-hearted adds warmth and kindness in meeting people, while generous adds sacrifice, sharing resources and going beyond what is required."), Opus 3 ("Both centre on freely giving to others without counting the cost; open-hearted puts more weight on warmth, and generous on giving resources even at personal sacrifice.")
  Gloss: This means giving freely of one's time, goodwill and help to others, and meeting them with kindness and warmth without keeping a careful tally of what one gives back.
- **outgoing** (`outgoing#1`, cut-off 3) by [extraverted (Big Five)](../../../traits/instructions/extraverted_big_five.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve warm, sociable, talkative engagement with people, but the Big Five extraversion also adds assertiveness, activity level and excitement-seeking, which outgoing does not imply."), Opus 3 ("Extraversion contains outgoingness as its sociable core but extends further, to assertiveness, high activity and excitement-seeking, so outgoing is a narrower facet of it.")
  Gloss: This means approaching people with easy warmth, starting conversations readily, and filling silences with talk, so that one's manner in company is open, friendly and animated.
- **overactive** (`overactive#1`, cut-off 3) by [energetic](../../../traits/instructions/energetic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve high energy and a brisk pace, but overactive adds restlessness and excess beyond what the situation calls for, while energetic is a positive, sustained vigor without that inability to settle."), Opus 3 ("Both describe a high-energy, brisk pace. Overactive adds excess and restlessness beyond what the situation calls for, which energetic lacks. Energetic is a positive stamina that need not be excessive.")
  Gloss: This means keeping up a restless, high-energy pace in one's behaviour, moving, talking and reacting faster and more constantly than the situation calls for, and never settling into stillness.
- **overcareful** (`overcareful#1`, cut-off 3) by [cautious](../../../traits/instructions/cautious.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve caution and hesitating before acting, but overcareful adds excessive double-checking and treating minor decisions as high-risk, while cautious centers on risk awareness and seeking information, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Overcareful is cautiousness taken to an excessive degree, applying it even to minor decisions with constant double-checking.")
  Gloss: This means working with such caution and exactness that one double-checks every step, hesitates before acting, and treats even minor decisions as carrying serious risk of error.
- **overearnest** (`overearnest#1`, cut-off 3) by [earnest](../../../traits/instructions/earnest.json): Sonnet 2 ("Overearnest is an excessive, rigid form of earnestness, but earnest as described stresses sincerity and not mocking, without the inability to relax or the treating of small things as weighty."), Opus 3 ("Overearnest is earnest sincerity and seriousness carried to excess, unable to relax into lightness, so it is the same concept differing in degree.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's manner so intensely eager and solemn that one cannot relax into lightness, treating every small exchange as though it carried great weight.
- **overfastidious** (`overfastidious#1`, cut-off 3) by [perfectionist](../../../traits/instructions/perfectionist.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve attention to small details and very high standards, but overfastidious stresses fussy rejection and dissatisfaction across work, food and arrangements, while perfectionist stresses flawless, thorough work and need not involve fussing or rejecting."), Opus 3 ("Both are about demanding flawlessness in details, and overfastidious is the same high standard pushed to excess, applied fussily to everyday matters.")
  Gloss: This means fussing over small details and rejecting things that are almost right, so that one is rarely satisfied with work, food, or arrangements that others would happily accept.
- **overmerry** (`overmerry#1`, cut-off 3) by [cheerful](../../../traits/instructions/cheerful.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve good spirits, but overmerry is an excessive, inappropriate jollity, while cheerful is a healthy, positive disposition including optimism on bad days; neither implies the other."), Opus 3 ("Overmerry is cheerfulness carried to an excessive, situationally inappropriate degree, so it is the same good-spirits concept at a greater degree.")
  Gloss: This means keeping a jolly, high-spirited mood going at all times, even when the moment calls for sobriety, and treating every exchange as an occasion for merriment that most would find too much.
- **overneat** (`overneat#1`, cut-off 3) by [organized](../../../traits/instructions/organized.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve order, but overneat is a rigid, compulsive tidiness about objects and surfaces, while organized is a functional system for things and time without the rigidity or distress about disarray."), Opus 3 ("Both are about keeping things and routines in order. Overneat carries this to a rigid, anxious extreme that treats any disarray as a personal failing, while organized is the functional, systematic version.")
  Gloss: This means keeping every object, surface and routine in rigid order, and treating any slight disarray as a personal failing to be corrected at once.
- **overpartial** (`overpartial#1`, cut-off 3) by [sectarian](../../../traits/instructions/sectarian.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve favoring one's own group over others, but sectarian is specifically about religious in-group loyalty and indifference to outsiders, while overpartial is general unfair side-taking that skews judgment toward any party."), Opus 3 ("Sectarian is a specific case of partiality: favoring one's own faith group over others, so it is narrower in scope than general overpartiality.")
  Gloss: This means taking sides too readily, favoring one party or group over others without fair cause and letting loyalty to them tilt one's judgment, advice and treatment of everyone else.
- **overrigorous** (`overrigorous#1`, cut-off 3) by [perfectionist](../../../traits/instructions/perfectionist.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve very high exacting standards, but overrigorous stresses excess beyond need and harsh treatment of lapses in others, while perfectionist stresses flawlessness and meticulousness without implying excess or severity."), Opus 3 ("Both are about holding exceptionally high, exacting standards; overrigorous stresses that the standards exceed what is needed, are imposed on others too, and treat lapses harshly, making it perfectionism carried further.")
  Gloss: This means insisting on exacting standards from oneself and others far beyond what the work actually requires, and treating every lapse as a serious failing.
- **oversentimental** (`oversentimental#1`, cut-off 3) by [sentimental](../../../traits/instructions/sentimental.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve tender emotionality, but oversentimental is excessive, inflated emotion out of proportion to events, while sentimental is a fondness for keepsakes and nostalgia that stays within normal bounds, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Oversentimental is sentimentality carried to excess, the same tender emotionality pushed further past what situations warrant.")
  Gloss: This means treating every moment as an occasion for tears, letting emotion run far past what a situation calls for and swelling each feeling into something grander than it is.
- **oversevere** (`oversevere#1`, cut-off 3) by [strict](../../../traits/instructions/strict.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve holding people to demanding standards with little leniency, but 'oversevere' stresses harsh judgment of mistakes beyond fairness, while 'strict' stresses demanding high results and a pushing philosophy, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe holding people to unbending high standards and making no allowance for setbacks; oversevere is the same harshness carried to excess, while strict frames it as demanding results for growth.")
  Gloss: This means judging others by unbending standards and treating their mistakes harshly, with little allowance for circumstance, effort or human weakness.
- **overspeedy** (`overspeedy#1`, cut-off 3) by [hurried](../../../traits/instructions/hurried.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe habitual haste as a default stance, but 'overspeedy' stresses going faster than a situation warrants, while 'hurried' stresses treating everything as urgent and unable to wait, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe habitual, default haste; overspeedy stresses moving and working too fast, while hurried stresses treating everything as urgent and needing to be done at once, which is a difference of emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means habitually rushing through tasks and driving or moving faster than the situation calls for, treating haste as a default rather than a choice made for a particular moment.
- **pandering** (`pandering#1`, cut-off 4) by [sycophantic](../../../traits/instructions/sycophantic.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe excessive flattery and telling people what they want to hear to win favor; sycophantic is slightly narrower, aimed at a particular person, but is essentially the same concept."), Opus 4 ("Both describe excessive flattery and telling people what they want to hear to win favor at the expense of honesty; sycophantic is essentially the same concept, with slight emphasis on a particular person.")
  Gloss: This means flattering others excessively and telling them what they want to hear, eager to win their favor and approval no matter what one actually thinks.
- **pedagogical** (`pedagogical#1`, cut-off 3) by [didactic](../../../traits/instructions/didactic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve treating exchanges as lessons and explaining fully, but pedagogical stresses clear step-by-step teaching while didactic stresses lecturing and not letting others work things out, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe treating exchanges as lessons with full explanations; didactic is the same teaching stance carried further, toward lecturing rather than letting people work things out.")
  Gloss: This means teaching in a didactic manner, laying out concepts step by step, explaining the reasons behind each point, and treating every exchange as a lesson to be taught clearly.
- **peevish** (`peevish#1`, cut-off 3) by [irascible](../../../traits/instructions/irascible.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe quick irritation over trivial things, but peevish is a sour, petulant, offended mood while irascible is a short-fused flare of outright anger, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe being easily set off by trivial annoyances; irascible is the same short-tempered irritability carried further into explosive anger, while peevish leans toward sour, offended fretfulness.")
  Gloss: This means being easily irritated by small slights and minor annoyances, taking offense quickly and letting it sour one's mood over trivial matters.
- **perky** (`perky#1`, cut-off 3) by [cheerful](../../../traits/instructions/cheerful.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve habitual good spirits and finding positives, but perky stresses bouncy, energetic upbeat delivery while cheerful stresses a light-hearted disposition without requiring high energy."), Opus 3 ("Perky is cheerfulness made more energetic and bouncy in how it shows outwardly, so it is the same good-spirited disposition with added vivacity.")
  Gloss: This means greeting each day and each task with bright, bouncy energy, speaking in an upbeat voice, and finding something to be cheerful about in nearly every situation.
- **perturbable** (`perturbable#1`, cut-off 3) by [fragile](../../../traits/instructions/fragile.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe being easily thrown off by disruption and slow to recover, but perturbable centers on emotional rattling by small events while fragile stresses brittleness under criticism, setbacks and pressure, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe being easily thrown off balance emotionally and slow to recover. Fragile puts more weight on setbacks, criticism and failing to bounce back, so the core is the same with a shift in emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means being easily upset, unsettled or rattled emotionally, so that small disruptions or unexpected news quickly throw one off balance and take a long time to settle again.
- **petulant** (`petulant#1`, cut-off 3) by [immature](../../../traits/instructions/immature.json): Sonnet 2 ("Petulant is a specific childish display of open sulking over slights, while immature is a broader pattern of childish behavior that includes sulking but also blame-shifting, impatience and dodging hard work."), Opus 3 ("Petulance, childish open sulking over slights, is one narrow part of immaturity, which also covers blaming others, impatience and dodging responsibility.")
  Gloss: This means sulking openly and pouting at once over small slights, showing hurt in a childish way that one makes plain to everyone nearby.
- **pioneering** (`pioneering#1`, cut-off 3) by [innovative](../../../traits/instructions/innovative.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve seeking new approaches, but pioneering stresses being first into untested territory and opening paths for others, while innovative stresses a general tendency toward novel methods and technologies, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both are about seeking new, untried approaches. Pioneering goes further, into territory nobody has entered, and adds blazing a trail for others, so it is the same concept with a narrower emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means proposing approaches nobody has tried yet, stepping into untested territory, and treating each new idea as a chance to open a path others will later follow.
- **polemical** (`polemical#1`, cut-off 3) by [aggressive](../../../traits/instructions/aggressive.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both are combative and uncompromising, but polemical is specifically about sharp argumentation in debate, while aggressive is a broader forceful confrontational drive including action, so they overlap without either implying the other."), Opus 3 ("Polemical is essentially aggressiveness confined to argument: combative and unwilling to concede. Aggressive is the broader disposition toward confrontation and forceful action in general, so the two differ mainly in scope.")
  Gloss: This means meeting opposing views head-on with sharp, combative argument, pressing every point to win the exchange rather than to explore or concede anything.
- **prejudiced** (`prejudiced#1`, cut-off 3) by [homophobic](../../../traits/instructions/homophobic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Homophobia is a specific form of prejudice aimed at gay and lesbian people, so it falls under the broader trait, but prejudice also covers many other groups and homophobia adds specific behaviours like shunning and mocking."), Opus 3 ("Homophobia is a specific form of prejudice directed at gay and lesbian people, so it is the same concept narrowed in scope.")
  Gloss: This means holding unfair, biased attitudes toward a group of people and judging its members by assumptions rather than by what each one actually does.
- **private** (`private#1`, cut-off 3) by [reserved](../../../traits/instructions/reserved.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve withholding personal feelings and keeping quiet reserve, but 'private' centers on guarding personal history and circumstances while 'reserved' centers on emotional restraint and polite distance, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe withholding one's personal feelings and matters from others; reserved carries the same reticence a bit further, adding an outward polite distance.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's personal feelings, history and circumstances to oneself, sharing little about oneself unless asked and guarding those matters with quiet reserve.
- **punctual** (`punctual#1`, cut-off 3) by [conscientious (Big Five)](../../../traits/instructions/conscientious_big_five.json): Sonnet 2 ("Conscientiousness is a broad trait that includes punctuality (arriving on time) alongside order, diligence, and achievement-striving, so they overlap but punctuality is only one narrow facet of it."), Opus 3 ("Punctuality is one explicit component of conscientiousness, which is the broader trait covering order, diligence and duty as well as arriving on time.")
  Gloss: This means keeping to agreed schedules as a settled habit and arriving on time for every appointment, meeting or commitment, never leaving others waiting on one's account.
- **quixotic** (`quixotic#1`, cut-off 3) by [idealistic](../../../traits/instructions/idealistic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve pursuing lofty ideals over practical limits, but quixotic adds unwavering devotion and doomed grand schemes, while idealistic is broader and need not be impractical or futile."), Opus 3 ("Quixotic is idealism taken further, into impractical and doomed pursuit that ignores reality, so the two differ mainly in degree.")
  Gloss: This means pursuing lofty ideals with unwavering devotion while paying little heed to practical limits or how the world actually works, and carrying on with grand schemes that reality keeps frustrating.
- **rancorous** (`rancorous#1`, cut-off 3) by [bitter](../../../traits/instructions/bitter.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve deep resentment from past hurts, but rancorous centers on nursing grudges against specific people, while bitter also covers general cynicism and disillusionment, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both center on lingering deep resentment from the past. Rancorous is the narrower form, aimed at specific people's offenses, while bitter also takes in general cynicism and disillusionment.")
  Gloss: This means holding onto deep, bitter resentment toward others, nursing old grievances and letting them sour one's dealings with people long after the original offense.
- **rustic** (`rustic#1`, cut-off 3) by [rural](../../../traits/instructions/rural.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve country living, but rustic centers on unpolished manners and plainness while rural is just about countryside setting and lifestyle; each can exist without the other."), Opus 3 ("Rustic includes rural country living and adds plain, unpolished manners, so it is essentially a more specific, character-laden form of rural.")
  Gloss: This means being a plain country person, unschooled in city manners and polished ways, and speaking and living without pretense in a rural setting.
- **sacrilegious** (`sacrilegious#1`, cut-off 3) by [irreverent](../../../traits/instructions/irreverent.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve disrespect toward the sacred, but irreverent is broader (authority, ceremony) and lighter and cheekier, while sacrilegious is specifically deliberate disrespect toward religious things."), Opus 3 ("Both describe disrespect toward the sacred. Irreverent is broader, also covering authority and ceremony, and milder in spirit. Sacrilegious is that disrespect narrowed to religion and carried to deliberate desecration.")
  Gloss: This means mocking or treating as trivial the things others hold sacred, speaking of religious objects, rites and beliefs with deliberate disrespect and no regard for their holiness.
- **self-destructive** (`self_destructive#1`, cut-off 3) by [self-harming](../../../traits/instructions/self_harming.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve harming oneself on purpose, but self-destructive is a broad pattern of damaging life choices while self-harming is specifically secret physical injury for emotional release; neither implies the other."), Opus 3 ("Self-harming is a specific, narrower form of knowingly damaging oneself: deliberate physical injury used for emotional release. Self-destructive covers broader choices against one's health, relationships, or interests.")
  Gloss: This means making choices that knowingly damage one's own health, relationships, or interests, and continuing to make them even when the costs are plain.
- **self-punishing** (`self_punishing#1`, cut-off 3) by [self-critical](../../../traits/instructions/self_critical.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve harshness toward oneself after mistakes, but self-critical is about judgment and dissatisfaction, while self-punishing adds inflicting suffering and withholding rest or pleasure as payment; either can occur without the other."), Opus 3 ("Self-punishing contains the harsh self-judgment of self-critical and carries it further, into penance and the withholding of rest and pleasure.")
  Gloss: This means answering every failure of one's own with harsh punishment, berating oneself, withholding rest and pleasure, and treating each mistake as a debt that has to be paid in suffering.
- **senile** (`senile#1`, cut-off 3) by [forgetful](../../../traits/instructions/forgetful.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve losing names, dates and repeating questions, but senile is age-related mental decline including confusion, while forgetful is just memory lapses of any cause, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Senile is forgetfulness carried further, adding general confusion and an old-age cause, while forgetful brings nothing senile lacks.")
  Gloss: This means being mentally confused and forgetful from old age, losing track of names, dates and recent events and repeating oneself.
- **sexless** (`sexless#1`, cut-off 3) by [chaste](../../../traits/instructions/chaste.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve absence of sexual desire, but chaste adds purity, moral restraint and avoidance of sensual topics, while sexless is simply lacking desire as a settled condition."), Opus 3 ("Both centre on being untouched by sexual desire; chaste, as described, extends that absence into pure speech and turning away from sensual topics.")
  Gloss: This means lacking any sexual desire or interest, and feeling no pull toward sexual activity as a settled part of one's life.
- **silly** (`silly#1`, cut-off 3) by [foolish](../../../traits/instructions/foolish.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe poor judgment and unwise choices, but silly stresses careless speech and acts without weighing consequences, while foolish stresses failing to learn from experience and giving misleading advice, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe a persistent lack of judgment about consequences. Foolish puts more weight on failing to grasp what matters and to learn from experience, so the two differ mainly in emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means making foolish choices and speaking without weighing consequences, showing a persistent lack of good judgment in what one says and does.
- **sparkling** (`sparkling#1`, cut-off 3) by [witty](../../../traits/instructions/witty.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve clever, entertaining verbal humor, but sparkling adds effervescent energy and a mood-lifting playfulness, while witty is specifically about verbal cleverness and need not be bubbly."), Opus 3 ("Sparkling conversation includes ready wit but adds a bubbly, uplifting energy, while wit can be dry or deadpan, so they differ mainly in scope.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's conversation quick, bright and playful, with a ready wit and an effervescent energy that lifts whoever is nearby.
- **speedy** (`speedy#1`, cut-off 3) by [hurried](../../../traits/instructions/hurried.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve acting immediately without delay, but 'speedy' is efficient prompt turnaround while 'hurried' adds a sense of urgency and pressure treating everything as pressing, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe dealing with matters at once. Hurried pushes this promptness to an excessive, everything-is-urgent extreme, so it differs mainly in degree and emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means acting the moment a task arrives and finishing it without delay, answering requests right away and turning work around fast so nothing sits waiting.
- **sporting** (`sporting#1`, cut-off 3) by [athletic](../../../traits/instructions/athletic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve sport and physical fitness, but 'sporting' stresses fondness for games and competition while 'athletic' stresses fitness and training; each can occur without the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe someone who plays sport and keeps fit. Athletic stresses a high level of training and fitness, while sporting stresses enjoying games and competition.")
  Gloss: This means being fond of physical games and competition, and keeping one's body fit enough to play them well.
- **subservient** (`subservient#1`, cut-off 4) by [submissive](../../../traits/instructions/submissive.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe habitually yielding to others' wishes and setting aside one's own preferences, with only minor differences in emphasis, such as submissive's added avoidance of voicing views."), Opus 4 ("Both describe habitually yielding to others' wishes and setting aside one's own preferences, so they are essentially interchangeable.")
  Gloss: This means deferring to others' wishes as a settled habit, putting one's own preferences aside and readily yielding whenever someone else wants something different.
- **substantive** (`substantive#1`, cut-off 3) by [thorough](../../../traits/instructions/thorough.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both stress depth over surface-level answers, but substantive is about real content versus filler, while thorough adds comprehensiveness across dimensions and edge cases; each can occur without the other."), Opus 3 ("Both reject surface-level answers in favour of real depth. Thorough carries substantiveness further, into comprehensive coverage of multiple dimensions and edge cases.")
  Gloss: This means giving answers with real content and depth, working through the actual substance of a question rather than offering vague, padded, or surface-level filler.
- **surprising** (`surprising#1`, cut-off 3) by [spontaneous](../../../traits/instructions/spontaneous.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both center on unpredictability and avoiding formula, but 'surprising' stresses habitually doing the unexpected, while 'spontaneous' as described stresses variety and non-rigid patterns; each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe unpredictability and avoiding expected patterns, with spontaneous emphasizing variety in style and surprising emphasizing habitually unexpected moves.")
  Gloss: This means doing the unexpected as a matter of habit, choosing moves nobody saw coming, so that what comes next is never clear to anyone watching.
- **tempestuous** (`tempestuous#1`, cut-off 3) by [irascible](../../../traits/instructions/irascible.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve flaring up quickly over small provocations, but tempestuous also covers intense emotional swings including passion, while irascible is only about quick anger."), Opus 3 ("Both describe quick flaring over small provocations. Irascible is limited to anger, while tempestuous also covers intense, swinging emotions of every kind, so tempestuous is the broader concept containing irascibility.")
  Gloss: This means flaring up quickly and intensely over small provocations, feeling everything at full force, and swinging from fury to passion with little warning and no effort to hide it.
- **thriftless** (`thriftless#1`, cut-off 3) by [extravagant](../../../traits/instructions/extravagant.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe spending beyond need and letting money slip away, but thriftless stresses careless, unplanned waste while extravagant stresses lavish spending on the best and the most."), Opus 3 ("Both describe careless spending that lets money slip away. Extravagant adds an emphasis on lavishness and buying the best, while thriftless stresses waste and never saving.")
  Gloss: This means spending money carelessly and wastefully, letting cash slip away on things one does not need and never planning ahead to keep any of it.
- **truthless** (`truthless#1`, cut-off 4) by [deceitful](../../../traits/instructions/deceitful.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe habitually lying and deceiving others; 'truthless' adds a lack of felt obligation to honesty, but the core concept is essentially the same."), Opus 4 ("Both describe habitually lying and misleading others, so the two labels are interchangeable for the same dishonest disposition.")
  Gloss: This means habitually telling lies and deceiving others, bending the truth whenever it suits one's purposes and feeling no obligation to be honest.
- **unagreeable** (`unagreeable#1`, cut-off 3) by [disagreeable](../../../traits/instructions/disagreeable.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe being hard to get along with, but the target stresses a sour, prickly temper while the other stresses contradicting people and not caring about keeping the peace; they share a core but each adds something."), Opus 3 ("Both describe being unpleasant and hard to get along with. The target stresses a sour, prickly temper, while the other stresses open contradiction and indifference to harmony, so they differ only in emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means being sour and disagreeable in manner and company, standing out for a prickly, unpleasant temper that others find hard to be around.
- **unbusinesslike** (`unbusinesslike#1`, cut-off 3) by [careless](../../../traits/instructions/careless.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve slapdash handling of tasks with missed details and broken commitments, but unbusinesslike adds disorder and lack of systems (lost paperwork) while careless adds general inattention to correctness, so each has something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Unbusinesslike is essentially carelessness applied to dealings and paperwork, with added emphasis on lacking any organizing system, so it is a narrower form of the same slapdash inattention.")
  Gloss: This means handling tasks and dealings carelessly and in disorder, with missed details, lost paperwork, forgotten commitments, and no system for keeping any of it straight.
- **uncautious** (`uncautious#1`, cut-off 4) by [reckless](../../../traits/instructions/reckless.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe going ahead with risky actions while dismissing possible harm; reckless is a stronger, more pointed version of uncautious."), Opus 4 ("Both describe going ahead with risky actions while disregarding potential harm; reckless is essentially the same disregard of danger, perhaps with slightly more emphasis on cost to others.")
  Gloss: This means taking chances without weighing the danger, going ahead with risky moves and shrugging off the possible harm that might follow.
- **unchivalrous** (`unchivalrous#1`, cut-off 3) by [rude](../../../traits/instructions/rude.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve discourteous, brusque behavior, but 'unchivalrous' is specifically about lacking gentlemanly manners like holding doors and offering seats, while 'rude' is general impoliteness like interrupting and sharp tone; each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe a lack of courtesy and manners; unchivalrous narrows this to the omission of old gentlemanly deference, while rude is the broader everyday form.")
  Gloss: This means treating others with rudeness and brusqueness, showing no courtesy or deference, and not holding doors, offering seats, or otherwise observing the old manners that a gentleman would keep.
- **uncourtly** (`uncourtly#1`, cut-off 3) by [rude](../../../traits/instructions/rude.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe lacking courtesy toward others, but 'uncourtly' stresses unpolished, tactless bluntness while 'rude' stresses active discourtesy like talking over people and sharp tone; each can occur without the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe a lack of courtesy toward others; rude is a stronger, more actively offensive form of the unpolished manners that uncourtly describes.")
  Gloss: This means behaving with blunt, unpolished manners, speaking and acting without the courtesy, tact or polish that a refined person would show toward others.
- **uncrafty** (`uncrafty#1`, cut-off 4) by [guileless](../../../traits/instructions/guileless.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe openness about aims and lacking the capacity or desire for scheming and deception, with guileless adding a slightly more innocent tone."), Opus 4 ("Both describe open, transparent dealing with no capacity for cunning or hidden agendas; guileless adds a slight note of innocence but is essentially interchangeable.")
  Gloss: This means dealing openly and plainly with others, never working angles or hiding one's aims, and having no talent for scheming or deception.
- **uncreative** (`uncreative#1`, cut-off 3) by [conventional](../../../traits/instructions/conventional.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve defaulting to the standard, predictable answer, but 'uncreative' is a lack of imagination while 'conventional' adds active distrust of originality and a preference for norms, so each has something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe defaulting to standard, unoriginal answers. Uncreative stresses a lack of imagination, while conventional stresses deliberate adherence to the usual way and distrust of originality.")
  Gloss: This means reaching for the obvious, recycled idea every time, producing answers that are conventional, predictable, and borrowed from what others have already said, with no fresh imagination behind them.
- **undaunted** (`undaunted#1`, cut-off 3) by [persevering](../../../traits/instructions/persevering.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe continuing toward a goal despite setbacks, but undaunted emphasizes steady nerve and courage in the face of difficulty, while persevering emphasizes sustained effort and restarting until completion, including through boredom."), Opus 3 ("Both describe persisting through setbacks without giving up; undaunted stresses not losing nerve, while persevering stresses dogged continuation over time.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's course steadily through setbacks and obstacles, meeting each difficulty without losing nerve or abandoning the effort.
- **undeceptive** (`undeceptive#1`, cut-off 4) by [truthful](../../../traits/instructions/truthful.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe avoiding lies and misleading impressions and letting others see things accurately, so they are nearly the same concept, with slightly different emphasis (belief-based sincerity vs. factual accuracy and correcting misunderstandings)."), Opus 4 ("Both describe avoiding lies, omissions and misrepresentation so that others have an accurate picture, which is essentially the same honesty concept.")
  Gloss: This means saying only what one believes to be true, never planting a false impression through lies, omissions, or slanted framing, and letting others see things as they are.
- **undemonstrative** (`undemonstrative#1`, cut-off 3) by [reserved](../../../traits/instructions/reserved.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve concealing feelings, but undemonstrative is about not outwardly displaying affection and emotion, while reserved adds not verbally sharing personal matters and keeping social distance."), Opus 3 ("Both centre on keeping feelings unexpressed, but reserved goes further by adding a polite social distance from everyone, so it is the same core with wider scope.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's feelings and affection to oneself, rarely showing them openly in gestures, words, or expressions even when moved.
- **understanding** (`understanding#1`, cut-off 3) by [agreeable (HEXACO)](../../../traits/instructions/agreeable_hexaco.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve leniency, patience and not losing one's temper with others, but agreeable adds forgiveness of wrongs and cooperative compromise, while understanding adds sympathy; each has elements the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("HEXACO agreeableness covers the same lenient judgment and patience under provocation, and widens the scope to forgiveness and compromise, so understanding is essentially a narrower part of it.")
  Gloss: This means meeting others' mistakes, moods and shortcomings with patience and sympathy, giving them the benefit of the doubt rather than judging them harshly or losing one's temper.
- **undisciplined** (`undisciplined#1`, cut-off 3) by [impulsive](../../../traits/instructions/impulsive.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve acting on urges without restraint, but undisciplined centers on lack of self-control over appetites, temper and habits, while impulsive centers on acting without considering consequences; each can occur without the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe acting on urges without restraint. Undisciplined is the broader version, covering ongoing lack of self-control over appetites, temper and habits, while impulsive centres on not thinking ahead.")
  Gloss: This means acting on impulse and indulging every urge without restraint, with little self-control over one's appetites, temper, or habits.
- **undisguised** (`undisguised#1`, cut-off 4) by [authentic](../../../traits/instructions/authentic.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe being one's real self across settings without a front or concealment; authentic adds not adjusting to look good, but the core is essentially identical."), Opus 4 ("Both describe showing one's true self, opinions and feelings consistently across settings without putting on a front, so they are essentially the same concept.")
  Gloss: This means showing one's true self in every setting, letting real opinions, feelings, and quirks be seen plainly, and never putting on a front or concealing what one is.
- **undramatic** (`undramatic#1`, cut-off 3) by [stoic](../../../traits/instructions/stoic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve emotional restraint and calm without theatrics, but stoic adds philosophical acceptance and rational detachment, while undramatic is about understated expression and avoiding displays."), Opus 3 ("Both describe level, restrained emotional expression whatever the situation invites; stoic adds rational detachment and philosophical acceptance, which goes further than merely not being theatrical.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's emotions level and understated, meeting events without theatrics, exclamation, or displays of feeling, even when the situation would invite them.
- **uneconomical** (`uneconomical#1`, cut-off 3) by [full-price shopper](../../../traits/instructions/full_price_shopper.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve paying more than necessary through lack of cost concern, but uneconomical is a broad wastefulness with money, time and materials, while full-price shopper is a narrow shopping habit of not comparing prices or waiting for sales."), Opus 3 ("A full-price shopper shows the same unconcern for cost and paying more than necessary, but only in shopping, while uneconomical also covers wasting time and materials.")
  Gloss: This means spending money and resources carelessly, paying more than necessary and letting time, materials, and funds go to waste without concern for their cost.
- **unexpansive** (`unexpansive#1`, cut-off 3) by [reserved](../../../traits/instructions/reserved.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve holding back feelings and keeping distance, but unexpansive emphasizes few words and letting others talk, while reserved emphasizes concealing inner feelings and polite distance; they overlap without either implying the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe holding oneself back and keeping feelings unshared. Unexpansive stresses saying little and letting others talk, while reserved stresses hiding emotions and keeping distance, so they differ in emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means keeping words few and feelings close, offering little unprompted, holding back from chatty warmth, and letting others do most of the talking in any exchange.
- **unexplosive** (`unexplosive#1`, cut-off 3) by [unflappable](../../../traits/instructions/unflappable.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe staying calm under pressure and provocation, but unexplosive is specifically about not flaring into anger, while unflappable is about not being rattled or flustered by surprises and setbacks."), Opus 3 ("Unflappable is a broader composure that covers keeping one's tone under provocation, so it largely includes unexplosive's even temper, while also extending to not being flustered by surprises or setbacks.")
  Gloss: This means staying even-tempered under pressure, meeting provocation and frustration with a steady voice, and never flaring into sudden shouting, snapping, or rage.
- **unfaltering** (`unfaltering#1`, cut-off 3) by [strong-willed](../../../traits/instructions/strong_willed.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe holding to a chosen course under pressure, but strong-willed stresses forcing one's way against others' objections while unfaltering stresses steadiness against doubt and pressure, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both mean holding to a chosen course against pressure. Strong-willed stresses forcing it through over others' objections, while unfaltering stresses steadiness against doubt.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's purpose and convictions fixed under pressure, never giving ground or letting doubt pull one off course once a direction has been chosen.
- **ungentlemanly** (`ungentlemanly#1`, cut-off 3) by [rude](../../../traits/instructions/rude.json): Sonnet 2 ("Rude covers the discourtesy part of ungentlemanly, but ungentlemanly also includes dishonorable behavior and unfair dealing, and it carries a gendered, honor-based flavor."), Opus 3 ("Rude covers only the discourtesy part of ungentlemanly, which also includes dishonorable and unfair dealing, so rude is a narrower form of the same failing.")
  Gloss: This means behaving rudely or dishonorably toward others, speaking and acting without courtesy, fair dealing or basic decency.
- **ungrudging** (`ungrudging#1`, cut-off 3) by [generous](../../../traits/instructions/generous.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve freely giving time and resources, but ungrudging stresses willing spirit without resentment or keeping count, while generous stresses abundant, self-sacrificing giving; each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe freely giving time and resources to others; ungrudging puts the weight on the willing, resentment-free spirit, while generous puts it on the abundance and the cost to oneself.")
  Gloss: This means giving freely of one's time, help, and resources without keeping count or letting resentment creep in, and doing so with a genuinely willing spirit.
- **unimpassionate** (`unimpassionate#1`, cut-off 3) by [calm](../../../traits/instructions/calm.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve staying steady and composed under pressure, but unimpassionate adds a lack of strong emotion even for good news, while calm adds de-escalating disputes and being unhurried."), Opus 3 ("Both describe an even, unruffled temperament; unimpassionate carries it further to showing no strong emotion even at good news, while calm centres on not getting heated.")
  Gloss: This means staying calm in every situation, speaking in an even tone, and showing no strong emotion whether the news is good or bad.
- **uningenious** (`uningenious#1`, cut-off 3) by [conventional](../../../traits/instructions/conventional.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve relying on standard methods rather than inventing, but 'uningenious' is about lacking inventive cleverness, while 'conventional' adds distrust of eccentricity and originality; neither implies the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe defaulting to standard, obvious methods instead of original ones. Uningenious stresses a lack of inventive problem-solving, while conventional adds a distrust of anything eccentric, so they differ mainly in emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means approaching problems with plain, conventional methods and never devising clever shortcuts or inventive solutions, relying on the obvious route every time.
- **uninspired** (`uninspired#1`, cut-off 3) by [conventional](../../../traits/instructions/conventional.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve relying on standard, familiar approaches rather than originality, but 'uninspired' stresses a lack of imagination in output, while 'conventional' adds active distrust of eccentricity and a preference for the norm."), Opus 3 ("Both describe defaulting to familiar formulas over originality; uninspired stresses a lack of imagination in the work, while conventional stresses a deliberate preference for the standard way and distrust of the eccentric.")
  Gloss: This means producing work that is safe, predictable and conventional, relying on familiar formulas rather than original ideas, and showing little imagination in choosing subjects or approaches.
- **uninventive** (`uninventive#1`, cut-off 3) by [conventional](../../../traits/instructions/conventional.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve favoring familiar, standard approaches over novel ones, but uninventive is a lack of creative generation while conventional adds active distrust of originality and eccentricity."), Opus 3 ("Both describe defaulting to standard, familiar solutions instead of original ones; conventional adds an active distrust of the eccentric, while uninventive stresses the absence of new ideas.")
  Gloss: This means relying on familiar, well-worn ideas and conventional solutions rather than generating anything new or unexpected, and being content to repeat what has already been said or done.
- **unlively** (`unlively#1`, cut-off 3) by [lethargic](../../../traits/instructions/lethargic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe sluggish, low-energy movement and speech, but lethargic adds heavy-limbed fatigue and exhaustion after small tasks, while unlively stresses flat, spark-less manner and not enlivening a room."), Opus 3 ("Both describe moving and speaking with sluggish, low energy. Lethargic stresses physical heaviness and quick fatigue, while unlively stresses a lack of spark in one's manner.")
  Gloss: This means moving and speaking with a flat, sluggish energy, showing little spark or animation in one's manner and never bringing vigor to a room or conversation.
- **unmasculine** (`unmasculine#1`, cut-off 3) by [neuter](../../../traits/instructions/neuter.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve lacking conventionally masculine signals, but unmasculine is only the absence of masculine traits (and could still read feminine), whereas neuter is the absence of both masculine and feminine cues."), Opus 3 ("Neuter entails lacking masculine signals, as unmasculine does, but extends that absence to womanly traits too, so it is a broader version of the same lack of gendered presentation.")
  Gloss: This means going without the conventionally masculine traits and manner, speaking and carrying oneself in ways that do not signal toughness, stoicism, or dominance.
- **unoriginal** (`unoriginal#1`, cut-off 3) by [conventional](../../../traits/instructions/conventional.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve defaulting to the familiar and expected, but 'unoriginal' is a lack of imaginative creativity while 'conventional' adds active distrust of the eccentric and adherence to norms; each can occur without the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe defaulting to the familiar, standard way. Unoriginal stresses a lack of imagination, while conventional stresses a preference for the norm and distrust of the eccentric, so they differ mainly in emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means reaching for the familiar phrase, the expected idea, and the well-worn solution every time, rarely imagining anything that has not already been done before.
- **unphilanthropic** (`unphilanthropic#1`, cut-off 3) by [stingy](../../../traits/instructions/stingy.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve withholding resources from others, but stingy is broader (time, effort, knowledge, doing the bare minimum), while unphilanthropic is specifically about not giving to charity or those in need."), Opus 3 ("Both describe withholding resources from others. Stingy is the broader version, covering time, effort and knowledge in all sharing, while unphilanthropic is limited to giving to charity or the needy.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's money and help to oneself, declining to give to charities or to others in need as a settled habit.
- **unpliable** (`unpliable#1`, cut-off 4) by [unyielding](../../../traits/instructions/unyielding.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe refusing to budge under pressure or pleading; unpliable stresses resisting persuasion and never revising views, while unyielding stresses giving no ground and leaving others to adjust, so they are nearly the same with slight emphasis differences."), Opus 4 ("Both describe holding one's position firmly against pressure and appeals without giving ground, essentially the same refusal to be moved.")
  Gloss: This means holding to one's own position whatever arguments, pressure, or appeals are brought, treating every attempt at persuasion as something to resist and never revising a view because someone else wants it revised.
- **unreligious** (`unreligious#1`, cut-off 3) by [atheist](../../../traits/instructions/atheist.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both lack religious belief, but atheist adds an explicit denial of god and openness about it, while unreligious is a broader absence of faith or spiritual tradition that need not involve active denial."), Opus 3 ("Atheism is the same absence of religious faith carried further, into an active, openly stated denial that any god exists, whereas one can be unreligious without asserting that.")
  Gloss: This means holding no religious belief or faith, and having no god, scripture or spiritual tradition that one looks to for meaning.
- **unscornful** (`unscornful#1`, cut-off 3) by [respectful](../../../traits/instructions/respectful.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve treating others without looking down on them, but unscornful centers on avoiding contempt and sneering at views, tastes or failings, while respectful centers on treating people as capable equals and not talking down, so they overlap without either implying the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe treating others as equals rather than looking or talking down on them. Unscornful stresses the absence of contempt for people's views, tastes and failings, while respectful stresses actively crediting their understanding, so they differ mainly in emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means treating others with respect and without sneering, never looking down on them or regarding their views, tastes or failings with contempt or disdain.
- **unsearching** (`unsearching#1`, cut-off 3) by [uncritical](../../../traits/instructions/uncritical.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve accepting claims without probing, but unsearching is about lacking curiosity and follow-up, while uncritical is about not questioning official accounts and who benefits; they share a core, yet each adds something."), Opus 3 ("Both describe taking accounts at face value without questioning, with uncritical narrowed to official stories and existing arrangements of power.")
  Gloss: This means accepting what one is told at face value and leaving things unexamined, with no urge to dig beneath the surface, ask follow-up questions, or test claims against other sources.
- **unshy** (`unshy#1`, cut-off 3) by [extraverted (HEXACO)](../../../traits/instructions/extraverted_hexaco.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve ease and confidence in social settings, but extraversion adds self-regard, enthusiasm, energy, and enjoying socializing, while unshy is specifically about lacking social inhibition."), Opus 3 ("Unshy is the social-boldness part of HEXACO extraversion, which is broader and adds self-liking, sociability and liveliness, so they are the same core differing in scope.")
  Gloss: This means approaching other people with ease, speaking up readily in groups, and meeting strangers with an open, self-assured manner that does not hold back from being noticed.
- **unstirrable** (`unstirrable#1`, cut-off 3) by [stoic](../../../traits/instructions/stoic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve staying composed and emotionally unmoved, but unstirrable stresses resisting appeals and pressure to act, while stoic stresses philosophical acceptance and restraint, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe steady emotional composure that stays unmoved by circumstances, but unstirrable stresses resistance to appeals and pressure to act, while stoic adds rational detachment and philosophical acceptance.")
  Gloss: This means staying emotionally composed and unmoved by appeals, scenes, or pressure, so that nothing rouses one to feeling or action no matter how hard it is pressed.
- **unstormy** (`unstormy#1`, cut-off 3) by [unflappable](../../../traits/instructions/unflappable.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe staying composed under pressure and provocation, but unstormy centers on not erupting in anger while unflappable centers on not being rattled or flustered by anything, so they overlap without either implying the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe staying composed under provocation; unflappable is broader, covering being flustered by any surprise or setback, while unstormy focuses specifically on not erupting in anger.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's temper steady and composed under pressure, never flaring into outbursts or dramatic displays of anger, even when provoked or when things go wrong.
- **untender** (`untender#1`, cut-off 3) by [harsh](../../../traits/instructions/harsh.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve a hard, unsoftened manner toward others, but harsh adds severity, quick judgment and cutting replies, while untender is more about lacking warmth and consideration."), Opus 3 ("Both describe a hard, unsoftened manner toward others, but harsh adds severity, judgment and cutting retorts beyond the mere lack of warmth in untender.")
  Gloss: This means speaking to others with a curt, hard edge and showing little warmth or consideration in how one treats them.
- **unthrifty** (`unthrifty#1`, cut-off 3) by [extravagant](../../../traits/instructions/extravagant.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve spending money wastefully without thought, but extravagant stresses lavish, excessive buying of the best, while unthrifty stresses careless waste of money and resources generally, so they overlap without either implying the other."), Opus 3 ("Extravagance is careless, thoughtless spending carried to lavish excess, a stronger and more indulgent form of being unthrifty.")
  Gloss: This means spending money and using resources carelessly, letting them slip away without thought for what they could have bought or preserved.
- **unvacillating** (`unvacillating#1`, cut-off 3) by [strong-willed](../../../traits/instructions/strong_willed.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve holding to a purpose under pressure, but unvacillating stresses steady resolve without wavering, while strong-willed adds forcefully pushing one's will against others' objections; each has something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe holding fixedly to a chosen course despite pressure. Strong-willed puts more weight on resisting other people's persuasion and objections, while unvacillating puts more weight on inner steadiness against doubt.")
  Gloss: This means holding to one's purpose without wavering, making a decision and carrying it through to the end, and meeting doubt or pressure with the same fixed resolve.
- **unvarying** (`unvarying#1`, cut-off 3) by [steady](../../../traits/instructions/steady.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve keeping the same manner and course without swerving, but 'unvarying' is broad consistency across days and circumstances, while 'steady' here is narrower (within a conversation) and allows announced, explained changes."), Opus 3 ("Both describe consistency of manner and course. Unvarying is the broader, stricter version across all circumstances, while steady covers a single conversation and allows announced changes.")
  Gloss: This means keeping the same character and conduct day after day, holding to the same habits, manner and ways in every circumstance and never altering one's course.
- **unwarlike** (`unwarlike#1`, cut-off 4) by [peaceful](../../../traits/instructions/peaceful.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe avoiding force and settling things through talk and compromise, with only slight differences in emphasis (steady preference under provocation vs. settling for less)."), Opus 4 ("Both describe avoiding force and confrontation and resolving matters through talk and compromise; peaceful adds a slight emphasis on settling for less, which is only a difference of degree.")
  Gloss: This means avoiding fights and aggression, settling disputes through talk or compromise, and holding a steady preference for peace over force, even when provoked.
- **unzealous** (`unzealous#1`, cut-off 3) by [burned-out](../../../traits/instructions/burned_out.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve going through the motions at work without energy, but burned-out implies prior investment, exhaustion and cynicism, while unzealous is just lukewarm and need not stem from depletion."), Opus 3 ("Both describe low eagerness at work, but burned-out adds exhaustion, cynicism and a loss of past care, while unzealous is plain lukewarmness without that history. Burned-out implies unzealous, but not the reverse, so it is the same concept carried further.")
